6
Summary and conclusions

In this paper,
as a preparation for our future study
aiming to develop data analysis procedures
using and/or combining 3-dimensional information
offered by directional Dark Matter detection experiments,
to, e.g., reconstruct the 3-dimensional WIMP velocity distribution,
we simulated 3-D velocity information
(magnitude, direction and measuring time)
of WIMP events in the Galactic coordinate system,
transformed them to different celestial coordinate systems,
and then
investigated the angular distribution patterns
of the 3-dimensional WIMP velocity
as well as
reconstructed its radial distribution (magnitude)
by applying the Bayesian fitting technique.

Our simulations indicate that,
first,
with
\cal O
(50) total WIMP events
recorded in one entire year,
the angular distribution patterns of the 3-D WIMP velocity
transformed to the Ecliptic and Equatorial coordinate systems
would already show clear anisotropy.
And,
more precisely,
the most frequent directions of the 3-D WIMP velocity
would be close to,
but somehow deviate from
the theoretically predicted direction of the WIMP wind,
i.e.,
the opposite direction of the Solar movement
in our Galaxy.
Once
\cal O
(500) total events could be accumulated
and thus a higher analysis resolution would be used,
the systematic bias could be reduced,
but a small deviation might still exist.

Moreover,
for demonstrating
the “annual” modulation of
the main direction of the WIMP wind
due to the Earth’s orbital motion around the Sun,
we considered two sets of observation periods:
four normal seasons
and four advanced seasons,

\cal O
(50) total recorded events in 60 observation days
(
\pm
30 days from the central date)
had been considered for each season.
It has been found that,
with only
\cal O
(50) total WIMP events
recorded in one season,
the pattern of the angular distribution
of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the Equatorial coordinate system
would already vary slightly but clearly
with a clockwise rotation
around the theoretical main direction of the WIMP wind.
This would be,
besides the pure anisotropy of the WIMP velocity,
a second (important) characteristic
for identifying directional WIMP signals
and discriminating from any (unexpected) backgrounds
with some specified incoming directions.
Meanwhile,
the angular distribution pattern
in our Earth coordinate system
in both of four normal and four advanced seasons
would also show clear anisotropy
with an annually rotation.

In addition,
in our laboratory–dependent horizontal coordinate system,
as expected,
our simulation results
with
\cal O
(50) total events
recorded in each (60-day) season
show clearly reversed distribution patterns
for laboratories located
in the Northern or the Southern Hemisphere.
The simulations for different underground laboratories
show also that,
with more WIMP events
and a higher analysis resolution,
we might even be able to demonstrate
a more detailed latitude–dependent pattern.

Regarding the original purpose
of directional Dark Matter detection experiments,
we demonstrated also
the laboratory–dependent diurnal modulation of
the angular WIMP velocity distribution pattern
in our laboratory coordinate system
with
\cal O
(50) total WIMP events
recorded in each 4-hour daily shift
(in the 60-day observation period).
Not surprisingly,
such a laboratory–dependent diurnal modulation
shows a similar variation pattern
to the annual modulation
in our horizontal coordinate system,
requires however much more recorded events.

On the other hand,
we found unexpectedly that,
with only
\cal O
(50) total WIMP events
recorded in one entire year,
the radial component of
the transformed 3-D WIMP velocity
would already show a distribution shape,
which is a little bit more concentrated
than the theoretically derived
shifted Maxwellian velocity distribution
in the range around the average WIMP velocity,
but a little bit reduced
in the lower and higher velocity ranges.
By using the Bayesian fitting technique,
we obtained the slightly–smaller best–fit values of
the fitting parameter
v_{0}

(under the constraint of
v_{\rm e}=1.05~\!v_{0}
)
and
v_{\rm e}

(under the constraint of
v_{0}=220
km/s),
although
the reconstructed velocity distributions could match
the theoretical velocity distribution very well.

However,
once we release the constraints on
v_{0}
and
v_{\rm e}

and treat them as
two independent fitting parameters,
the reconstructed velocity distributions
would have a clearly different shape
from the theoretical prediction,
although,
with only
\cal O
(50) total events
recorded in one entire year
and thus a relatively large statistical uncertainties,
the 1
\sigma
statistical uncertainty bands
of the reconstructed velocity distributions
could still cover
the theoretical distribution
in most part of the velocity range.
The best–fit values of
the fitting parameters
v_{0}
and
v_{\rm e}

would also be
\sim
16%
smaller and
\sim
14% larger
than their theoretical values,
respectively.

Once we raise the event number to

\cal O
(500) total WIMP events,
the best–fit velocity distributions
would differ pretty strongly
from the theoretical distribution
and
their narrower 1
\sigma
statistical uncertainty bands
could not cover the expected curve
in most part of the velocity ranges;
the fitting parameters
v_{0}
and
v_{\rm e}

would also be 3
\sigma
– 4
\sigma

smaller or larger
than their theoretical values,
respectively.
Additionally,
our reconstructed radial distributions
as well as
their 1(2)
\sigma
statistical uncertainty bands
seem not to fit
the radial distribution of the simulated 3-D WIMP velocity
in the middle velocity range very well.
These would imply not only that
the radial distribution of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the Equatorial as well as the in laboratory coordinate systems
would differ from the theoretically derived analytic form,
but also
a requirement of the modification of the analytic form of
the fitting velocity distribution function.

Nevertheless,
with
\cal O
(50) total WIMP events
recorded in each (60-day) season,
the annual modulation of
the radial distribution of the 3-D WIMP velocity
could already be identified very clearly
by observing
the
\sim
5% annual variation of the best–fit values of
the second fitting parameter
v_{\rm e}
.
In contrast and interestingly,
the best–fit values of the first fitting parameter
v_{0}

in all observation periods stay fixed,
although it is also unconstraint.

In our simulations presented in this paper,
50 and 500 total WIMP events on average
in (one daily shift of) one observation period
(365 days/year,
60 days/season,
or 4 hours/shift
\times
60 days)
have been considered.
In this and probably the next decades,
even only
\cal O
(50) total WIMP signals (per year) to observe
would be a strong challenge
for all (non)directional
direct Dark Matter detection experiments.
Fortunately,
the recorded 3-D velocity information of WIMP signals
offered by different underground laboratories
could (in principle) be
transformed to all laboratory–independent
(Galactic,
Ecliptic,
Equatorial,
and Earth)
coordinate systems.
Hence,
data offered by more than one directional DM detection experiment
could be combined for:

•

the identification of
the anisotropy of
the angular velocity distribution
of incident WIMPs;

•

the demonstration of
the annual modulation of
the angular velocity distribution pattern
of incident WIMPs;

•

the Bayesian reconstruction of
the radial distribution of the 3-D WIMP velocity;

•

the demonstration of
the annual modulation of
the (Bayesian reconstructed)
radial distribution of the 3-D WIMP velocity.

These analyses could be applied for reconstructing
the 3-dimensional WIMP velocity distribution
in both of the Equatorial and Galactic coordinate systems,
for checking our theoretical models of the Galactic Dark Matter halo.

In the long term,
with enough WIMP events
observed in one underground laboratory,
the angular distribution patterns of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the laboratory–dependent (horizontal and laboratory)
coordinate systems
could be used to demonstrate:

•

the diurnal modulation of
the angular velocity distribution pattern
of incident WIMPs;

•

the latitude–dependence of
the angular velocity distribution pattern
of incident WIMPs.

Regarding the observation periods
considered in our simulations
presented in this paper,
we used several approximations
about the Earth’s orbital motion
in the Solar system.
First,
the Earth’s orbit around the Sun is perfectly circular
on the Ecliptic plane
and the orbital speed is thus a constant.
Second,
the date of the vernal equinox is fixed exactly
at the end of the 79th day (the May 20th) of
a 365-day year
and the few extra hours
in an actual Solar year
have been neglected.
Nevertheless,
considering the very low WIMP scattering event rate
and thus only a few tens of total (combined) WIMP events
required in at least a few tens of days
(an overall event rate of
\>\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textstyle<}}{{\sim}}$}\>~\!1
event/day)
for the first–phase analyses,
these approximations should be acceptable.

For our simulations presented in this paper,
we also assumed implicitly that
3-D information on the velocity of incident WIMPs
(magnitudes and directions/angles)
can be measured
by using data
from directional detection experiments.
However,
so far we could only measure,
besides recoil energies,
recoil tracks and in turn recoil angles
of the recoiled nuclei.
A way to use the experimental measurable data
(recoil energies and recoil tracks/angles
of the recoiled nuclei)
to reconstruct 3-D WIMP velocities
requires more investigations.

Furthermore,
for generating the 3-D WIMP velocity
we considered in this paper only
the simplest isotropic Maxwellian velocity distribution.
Effects of
other theoretically predicted structures of
the Galactic Dark Matter halo,
including some possible streams
and/or the bulk rotation
discussed in literature,
and the possibility of distinguishing these models
would also be interesting to investigate.

In summary,
we have begun to extend our earlier works and to study
how to use information provided by
directional Dark Matter detection experiments
to understand
the 3-dimensional (velocity) distribution of
Galactic DM particles.
Hopefully,
this and more works achieved in the future
could offer useful information
on the structure of our Galaxy
as well as
for data analyses
in indirect Dark Matter detection experiments.

Appendix A
Definitions of and transformations between the celestial coordinate systems

In this section,
we describe the definitions of
the celestial coordinate systems
used in our simulations and data analyses
presented in this paper
as well as
the transformation matrices
between these coordinate systems
in detail.
In order to avoid possible confusion,
the conventional astronomical definitions
of these coordinate systems
will also be mentioned.

A.1
Definitions of the horizontal and the laboratory coordinate systems

Firstly,
in order to connect
the celestial (laboratory–independent)
(Galactic,
Ecliptic,
and Equatorial)
coordinate systems
with
the geographic (laboratory–dependent)
(horizontal and laboratory)
coordinate systems,
we defined particularly
the Earth coordinate system
as described in Sec.
2.1.3

and sketched in Fig.
4

[
64
]
.

Then we discuss here
two coordinate systems
depending on the geographic location of
the underground laboratory of interest
as well as
the measuring time of WIMP scattering events.

A.1.1
Conventional definition

Figure A1
:

The sketch of
the conventional definition of
the (dark–green) horizontal/laboratory coordinate system:
it is basically the same as our definition of
the horizontal coordinate system
described in Sec.
2.1.4

and sketched in Fig.
5
,
except that
the primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm H/Lab}
–axis)
points here towards east.
Our (light–green)
Earth coordinate system
is also given here.

Conventionally,
as shown in Fig.
A1
,
the horizontal/laboratory coordinate system
is defined with
the origin at the geographic location
of the laboratory of interest
at, e.g., 12 midnight (the beginning) of
(the UTC time of)
each single Solar day,
the primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm H/Lab}
–axis)
and the
{\bf Z}_{\rm H/Lab}
–axis
pointing towards east
and the zenith,
respectively,
and the right–handed convention
for defining the
{\bf Y}_{\rm H/Lab}
–axis.

In Fig.
A1
,
we sketch
the conventionally defined
horizontal/laboratory coordinate system
with our Earth coordinate system
together.
It can easily find out that,
by rotating the conventional horizontal/laboratory
coordinate system
at first
-\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)

around the
{\bf X}_{\rm H/Lab}
–axis
and then
-\left(\pi/2+\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)

around the
{\bf Z}_{\rm H/Lab}
–axis,
one can obtain our Earth coordinate system.
Hence,
the transformation matrix
from the conventional horizontal/laboratory coordinate system
to our Earth coordinate system
can be expressed by

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm H\to E,conv}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\left[-\left(\pi/2+\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]&~\sin\left[-\left(\pi/2+\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]&~0\\
-\sin\left[-\left(\pi/2+\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]&~\cos\left[-\left(\pi/2+\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]&~0\\
0&~0&~1\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt~~~~~~\times\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}1&~0&~0\\
0&~\cos\left[-\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]&~\sin\left[-\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]\\
0&~-\sin\left[-\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]&~\cos\left[-\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)\right]\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}&~-\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}&~-\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
0&~\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

where

\phi_{\rm Lab}
and
\theta_{\rm Lab}

are the longitude and the latitude of
the location of the laboratory,
respectively.
Conversely,
the transformation matrix
from our Earth coordinate system
to the conventional horizontal/laboratory coordinate system
can be given directly as

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm E\to H,conv}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm H\to E,conv}^{\rm T}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}&~0\\
-\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~-\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

A.1.2
Our definitions

In order to understand and compare
the angular distribution patterns
of the 3-D WIMP velocity
offered by different underground laboratories
more easily,
as sketched in Fig.
5
,
for our simulations presented in this paper
we defined the horizontal coordinate system
by rotating the conventional coordinates 90
∘

around the
{\bf Z}_{\rm H(/Lab)}
–axis,
so that
the
{\bf X}_{\rm H}
–axis points now towards north.
Remind that,
as our Earth coordinate system,
for each single Solar day,
our horizontal coordinate system
is fixed at 12 midnight (the beginning) of
(the UTC time of)
the day.

By this definition,
the transformation
from our horizontal coordinate system
to the Earth coordinate system
can be done by rotating
at first
\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}
around the
{\bf Y}_{\rm H}
–axis
and then
\pi-\phi_{\rm Lab}
around the
{\bf Z}_{\rm H}
–axis.
Thus
the transformation matrix
can be given by

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm H\to E}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\left(\pi-\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)&~\sin\left(\pi-\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)&~0\\
-\sin\left(\pi-\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)&~\cos\left(\pi-\phi_{\rm Lab}\right)&~0\\
0&~0&~1\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt~~~~~~\times\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)&~0&~-\sin\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)\\
0&~1&~0\\
\sin\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)&~0&~\cos\left(\pi/2-\theta_{\rm Lab}\right)\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
-\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~-\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~0&~\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

and then,
conversely,
we have

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm E\to H}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm H\to E}^{\rm T}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&-\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}&-\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}&0\\
\cos\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&\sin\phi_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

On the other hand,
considering the long running time of
(directional) direct Dark Matter detection experiments
as well as
for identifying the diurnal modulation of
the angular WIMP velocity distribution,
we also defined
the laboratory coordinate system
by taking into account
the instantaneous measuring time of
each recorded WIMP scattering event.
As sketched in Fig.
6
,
it is defined
by rotating our horizontal coordinate system
of the laboratory of interest
around the Earth’s north polar (
{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}/{\bf Z}_{\rm E}
–)axis instantaneously
by the angle of
\omega t_{\rm PM}
,
where we define

\omega\equiv\frac{2\pi}{1~{\rm day}}~\!,

(
1
)

and
t_{\rm PM}
indicates
the fractional part of
the measuring UTC time
t
of each recorded WIMP event
in unit of day.
Hence,
by adding the longitude of the laboratory of interest
in the transformation matrices
(
A.1.2
) and (
A.1.2
)
with this extra time–dependent term:

\phi_{\rm Lab}\longrightarrow\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}~\!,

(A31)

we can obtain the transformation matrices
between the laboratory and the Earth coordinate systems
straightforwardly as

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Lab\to E}(t,\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-\cos\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)&~\cos\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
-\sin\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~-\cos\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)&~\sin\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~0&~\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

and

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm E\to Lab}(t,\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-\cos\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~-\sin\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\sin\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)&~-\cos\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)&~0\\
\cos\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\left(\phi_{\rm Lab}+\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

Moreover,
by combining Eq. (
A.1.2
)
with Eq. (
A.1.2
),
the transformation matrices
between the horizontal and the laboratory coordinate systems
can be found as

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm H\to Lab}(t,\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm E\to Lab}(t,\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})~\!{\bf M}_{\rm H\to E}(\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\sin^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}+\cos^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\left(1-\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
-\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}&~\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\left(1-\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~-\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\cos^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}+\sin^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

and

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Lab\to H}(t,\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm H\to Lab}^{\rm T}(t,\phi_{\rm Lab},\theta_{\rm Lab})

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\sin^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}+\cos^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}&~-\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\left(1-\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}&~-\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\left(1-\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\right)\sin\theta_{\rm Lab}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\sin\omega t_{\rm PM}\cos\theta_{\rm Lab}&~\cos\omega t_{\rm PM}\cos^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}+\sin^{2}\theta_{\rm Lab}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

Note that
the transformations
between the horizontal and the laboratory coordinate systems
are longitude (
\phi_{\rm Lab}
) independent and
depend in fact only on
the latitude of the laboratory location
\theta_{\rm Lab}

(and the measuring time
t
).

A.2
Definitions of the Ecliptic and the Equatorial coordinate systems

Now we turn to define
the most important coordinate systems
in our data analysis procedures:
the Ecliptic and the Equatorial coordinate systems.
The transformation matrices
between the Earth and these two coordinate systems
will be derived in detail here.

A.2.1
Conventional definitions

Figure A2
:

The sketch of
the astronomical definitions of
the (red) Ecliptic and
the (blue) Equatorial coordinate systems:
they are basically the same as our definitions
described in Sec.
2.1.2

and sketched in Fig.
3
(a),
except that
the common primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm S}
/
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
–axis)
is now the direction
pointing from the Earth’s center to that of the Sun
at 12 noon of the date of the vernal equinox
(i.e.,
pointing towards the celestial Equinox),
and their
{\bf Y}_{\rm S}
– and
{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}
–axes
are then defined as usual by
the right–handed convention.

As shown in Fig.
A2
,
in astronomy
the Ecliptic and the Equatorial coordinate systems
are conventionally defined as follows:
their origins are located at
the center of the Sun
and that of the Earth,
respectively,
the common primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm S}
/
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
–axis)
is now the direction
pointing from the Earth’s center to that of the Sun
at 12 noon of the date of the vernal equinox
(i.e.,
pointing towards the celestial Equinox),
the fundamental (
{\bf X}_{\rm S}-{\bf Y}_{\rm S}
and
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}-{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}
) planes
are the Ecliptic plane
and the Equatorial plane,
respectively,
the
{\bf Z}_{\rm S}
– and
{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}
–axes
are perpendicular to the Ecliptic
or the Equatorial plane,
respectively,
and their
{\bf Y}_{\rm S}
– and
{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}
–axes
are then defined as usual by
the right–handed convention.

A.2.2
Our definitions

Our definitions of
the Ecliptic and
the Equatorial coordinate systems
are described in Sec.
2.1.2

and sketched in Fig.
3
(a).
Note only that,
firstly,
the Equatorial coordinate system
is fixed and moves with the Earth,
during the Earth’s orbital motion around the Sun
combined with
the motion of the Solar system in the Galaxy.
Secondly,
since in our definitions
the common primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm S}
/
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
–axis)
point towards the opposite direction
of the conventional astronomical defintion,
in our calculations given in Appendix
A.3

there is always a 180
∘

(or
-90^{\circ}
)
difference from the conventional data
provided by, e.g., Ref.
[
63
]

(and those used in Ref.
[
64
]
).

Figure A3
:

The sketch of
the relations between
the (light–green) Earth,
the (blue) Equatorial,
and the (red) Ecliptic coordinate systems
(cf. Fig.
4
).
Here

\psi_{\rm yr}

indicates the angle swept by the connection
between the Solar and the Earth’s centers,

{\bf r}_{\rm yr}
,
from the day of the vernal equinox,

\psi_{\oplus}=23.4^{\circ}

is the Earth’s obliquity,
and
\psi_{\rm PM}

is the angle
between the
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
– and the
{\bf X}_{\rm E}
–axes.
Other notations are the same as
in Fig.
4
.

In Fig.
A3
,
we sketch
the relations between
the Earth,
the Equatorial,
and the Ecliptic coordinate systems
(cf. Fig.
4
).
Here

\psi_{\rm yr}

indicates the angle swept by the connection
between the Solar and the Earth’s centers,

{\bf r}_{\rm yr}
,
from the day of the vernal equinox,

\psi_{\oplus}=23.4^{\circ}

is the Earth’s obliquity,
and
\psi_{\rm PM}

is the angle
between the
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
– and the
{\bf X}_{\rm E}
–axes.
It can be found that,
firstly,
the Equatorial coordinate system
can be obtained by simply rotating
the Ecliptic coordinate system
by the Earth’s obliquity
\psi_{\oplus}

around the common
{\bf X}_{\rm S}
/
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
–axis.
Hence,
the transformation matrix
from the Ecliptic
to the Equatorial coordinate system
can be given directly as

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}1&~0&~0\\
0&~\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~\sin\psi_{\oplus}\\
0&~-\sin\psi_{\oplus}&~\cos\psi_{\oplus}\\
\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}1&~0&~0\\
0&~0.91775&~0.39715\\
0&~-0.39715&~0.91775\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

and,
conversely,

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to S}={\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}^{\rm T}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}1&~0&~0\\
0&~\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~-\sin\psi_{\oplus}\\
0&~\sin\psi_{\oplus}&~\cos\psi_{\oplus}\\
\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}1&~0&~0\\
0&~0.91775&~-0.39715\\
0&~0.39715&~0.91775\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

(A62b)

On the other hand,
following the calculations
done by A. Bandyopadhyay and D. Majumdar
in Ref.
[
64
]
,
since
{\bf r}_{\rm yr}
is the intersection vector
of the (purple)
{\bf X}_{\rm E}-{\bf Z}_{\rm E}
plane
and the (yellow) Ecliptic (
{\bf X}_{\rm S}-{\bf Y}_{\rm S}
) plane,
one has

\displaystyle{\bf r}_{\rm yr}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm S}+\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm S}

(A63)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}-\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\sin\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}~\!,

and the vector perpendicular to
the (purple)
{\bf Z}_{\rm E}-{\bf X}_{\rm E}-{\bf r}_{\rm yr}
plane
(i.e.,
paralle to the
{\bf Y}_{\rm E}
–axis)
can be obtained by

\displaystyle{\bf Z}_{\rm E}\times{\bf r}_{\rm yr}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}\times\Big(\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}-\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\sin\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}\Big)

(A64)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}-\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}~\!.

Here
the angle
\psi_{\rm yr}
can be estimated
by the measuring time
t
of one WIMP event as

\psi_{\rm yr}(t)\equiv\frac{2\pi}{365}\Big[\left(t-t_{\rm PM}\right)-79.0\Big]~\!.

(A65)

Then
the vector perpendicular to
the
{\bf Y}_{\rm E}-{\bf Z}_{\rm E}
plane
(i.e.,
paralle to the
{\bf X}_{\rm E}
–axis)
can be given by

\displaystyle\Big({\bf Z}_{\rm E}\times{\bf r}_{\rm yr}\Big)\times{\bf Z}_{\rm E}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\Big(\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}-\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}\Big)\times{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}

(A66)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}~\!,

and its length can thus be obtained as

\left|\Big({\bf Z}_{\rm E}\times{\bf r}_{\rm yr}\Big)\times{\bf Z}_{\rm E}\right|=\sqrt{\cos^{2}\psi_{\rm yr}+\sin^{2}\psi_{\rm yr}\cos^{2}\psi_{\oplus}}~\!.

(A67)

Hence,
the unit vector
{\bf X}_{\rm E}
of
the Earth coordinate system
can be expressed as

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{\rm E}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\frac{\left({\bf Z}_{\rm E}\times{\bf r}_{\rm yr}\right)\times{\bf Z}_{\rm E}}{\left|\left({\bf Z}_{\rm E}\times{\bf r}_{\rm yr}\right)\times{\bf Z}_{\rm E}\right|}

(A68a)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\frac{\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}}{\sqrt{\cos^{2}\psi_{\rm yr}+\sin^{2}\psi_{\rm yr}\cos^{2}\psi_{\oplus}}}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}~\!,

and thus

\displaystyle{\bf Y}_{\rm E}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf Z}_{\rm E}\times{\bf X}_{\rm E}

(A68b)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf Z}_{\rm Eq}\times\Big(\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}-\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}~\!,

where we define

\gamma\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{\cos^{2}\psi_{\rm yr}+\sin^{2}\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos^{2}\psi_{\oplus}}}~\!.

(A69)

Therefore,
the transformation matrix
from the Equatorial
to the Earth coordinate system
can be given by

{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to E}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}&~\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~0\\
-\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}&~0\\
0&~0&~1\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

(A70a)

and,
conversely,

{\bf M}_{\rm E\to Eq}(t)={\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to E}^{\rm T}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}&~-\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~0\\
\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}&~0\\
0&~0&~1\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

(A70b)

Finally,
by combining the matrices
given in Eqs. (
A70a
) and (
A.2.2
),
the transformation matrix
from the Ecliptic
to the Earth coordinate system
can be obtained by

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm S\to E}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to E}(t)~\!{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}&~\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos^{2}\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\sin\psi_{\oplus}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}\\
-\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\sin\psi_{\oplus}\\
0&~-\sin\psi_{\oplus}&~\cos\psi_{\oplus}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

and,
conversely,

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm E\to S}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm S\to E}^{\rm T}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}&~-\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~0\\
\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos^{2}\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~-\sin\psi_{\oplus}\\
\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\sin\psi_{\oplus}~\!\cos\psi_{\oplus}&~\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!\sin\psi_{\oplus}&~\cos\psi_{\oplus}\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

A.2.3
Velocity of the Earth in the Ecliptic coordinate system

The orbital velocity of the Earth’s rotation around the Sun
in the Ecliptic coordinate system
can be expresses as

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\oplus,S}\Big(-\sin\psi_{\rm yr}(t)~\!{\bf X}_{\rm S}+\cos\psi_{\rm yr}(t)~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm S}\Big)=v_{\rm\oplus,S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-\sin\psi_{\rm yr}(t)\\
\cos\psi_{\rm yr}(t)\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

where
the Earth’s orbital speed
can be estimated by

[
65
]

v_{\rm\oplus,S}=\frac{2\pi\times 1.495978707\times 10^{8}~{\rm km}}{3.15569252\times 10^{7}~{\rm s}}\simeq 29.79~{\rm km/s}~\!.

(A83)

Note that
the Earth’s orbit around the Sun
has been assumed here to be perfectly circular
on the Ecliptic plane
and the orbital speed is thus a constant.

A.3
Definition of the Galactic coordinate system

Finally,
we come to define the Galactic coordinate system.
The Earth’s velocity relative to the Dark Matter halo
in the Ecliptic and the Equatorial coordinate systems
and its annual modulation
in four normal and four advanced seasons
given in Table
2

will also be discussed here in detail.

A.3.1
Conventional definition

Figure A4
:

The sketch of
the astronomical definitions of
the (black) Galactic coordinate system
in both of the Cartesian and the spherical coordinates
with
the origin located at the center of the Sun
(not the Galactic Center).
The primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm G}
–axis)
points from the Solar center to
the approximate center of the our Galaxy,
the
{\bf Z}_{\rm G}
–axis
points to the Galactic North Pole,
and
the
{\bf Y}_{\rm G}
–axis is then defined as usual
by the right–handed convention

[
63
]
.
On the other hand,
the Galactic longitude (
l
)
is measured from the
{\bf X}_{\rm G}
–axis
on the Galactic plane,
while
the Galactic latitude (
b
)
measures the angle of the object
above or below the approximate Galactic plane

[
63
]
.

In Fig.
A4
,
we sketch
the astronomical definitions of
the Galactic coordinate system
in both of the Cartesian and the spherical coordinates
with
the origin located at the center of the Sun
(not the Galactic Center).
The primary direction (the
{\bf X}_{\rm G}
–axis)
points from the Solar center to
the approximate center of the our Galaxy,
the
{\bf Z}_{\rm G}
–axis
points to the Galactic North Pole,
and
the
{\bf Y}_{\rm G}
–axis is then defined as usual
by the right–handed convention

[
63
]
.
On the other hand,
the Galactic longitude (
l
)
is measured from the
{\bf X}_{\rm G}
–axis
on the Galactic plane,
while
the Galactic latitude (
b
)
measures the angle of the object
above or below the approximate Galactic plane

[

.

A.3.2
Our definition

Our definition of
the Galactic coordinate system
is described in Sec.
2.1.1

and sketched in Fig.
2
.
Then,
according to Ref.
[
63
]
,
the direction of the Galactic Center
in the Equatorial coordinate system
can be expressed by

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{\rm G,Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}&~\cos\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}&~\sin\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cong

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.05495&~0.87340&~-0.48389\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

where
we have adopted the values
provided by Ref.
[
63
]
18
18

18

Remind that,
in the Ecliptic and the Equatorial coordinate systems
defined in Sec.
2.1.2
,
the common
{\bf X}_{\rm S}
/
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
–axis
points from the center of the Sun to that of the Earth,
which is opposite to the
\bf X
direction
defined conventionally.
Hence,
the right ascensions of the Galactic Center
and the Galactic North Pole
in the Equatorial and the Ecliptic coordinate systems
adopted in this section
as well as
those calculated later
would differ from the conventional values
by
12^{\rm h}=180^{\circ}
.

\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}=5^{\rm h}45.6^{\rm m}=86.40^{\circ}~\!,

(A88a)

and

\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}=-28.94^{\circ}~\!,

(A88b)

as the right ascension and the declination of the Galactic Center
in the Equatorial coordinate system
(from the
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}
(
={\bf X}_{\rm S}
)–axis
and the Earth’s/celestial Equator (
{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}-{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}
) plane),
respectively.
Meanwhile,
the direction of the Galactic North Pole
in our Equatorial coordinate system
can also be given as

[
63
]

\displaystyle{\bf Z}_{\rm G,Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}\cos\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}&~\cos\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}&~\sin\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cong

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.86769&~0.19793&~0.45601\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

where we have

\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}=51.4^{\rm m}=12.85^{\circ}~\!,

(A93a)

and

\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}=27.13^{\circ}~\!,

(A93b)

as the right ascension and the declination of the Galactic North Pole
in the Equatorial coordinate system,
respectively.
By combining Eqs. (
A.3.2
) and (
A.3.2
),
the
{\bf Y}_{\rm G}
–axis of the Galactic coordinate system
in the Equatorial coordinate system
can be calculated by

\displaystyle{\bf Y}_{\rm G,Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf Z}_{\rm G,Eq}\times{\bf X}_{\rm G,Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}\sin\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}-\sin\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\cos\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}\\
\sin\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\cos\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}-\cos\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}\sin\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\\
\cos\theta_{\rm GNP,Eq}\cos\theta_{\rm GC,Eq}\left(\cos\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}-\sin\phi_{\rm GNP,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm GC,Eq}\right)\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}^{\rm T}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cong

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}-0.49406&~0.44492&~0.74696\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!.

Then
the transformation matrix
from the Equatorial
to the Galactic coordinate system
can be given by

{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to G}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf X}_{\rm G,Eq}\\
{\bf Y}_{\rm G,Eq}\\
{\bf Z}_{\rm G,Eq}\\
\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.05495&~0.87340&~-0.48389\\
-0.49406&~0.44492&~0.74696\\
0.86769&~0.19793&~0.45601\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

(A100a)

and,
conversely,
we have

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm G\to Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to G}^{\rm T}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.05495&~-0.49406&~0.86769\\
0.87340&~0.44492&~0.19793\\
-0.48389&~0.74696&~0.45601\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

Furthermore,
by using the transformation matrices
between the Equatorial and the Ecliptic coordinate systems
in Eqs. (
A62b
) and (
A.2.2
),
the transformation matrices
between the Ecliptic and the Galactic coordinate systems
can be given by

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm G\to S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to S}{\bf M}_{\rm G\to Eq}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.05495&~-0.49406&~0.86769\\
0.99374&~0.11168&~0.00055\\
-0.09723&~0.86223&~0.49711\\
\end{array}\right]~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\rm S\to G}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm G\to S}^{\rm T}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.05495&~0.99374&~-0.09723\\
-0.49406&~0.11168&~0.86223\\
0.86769&~0.00055&~0.49711\\
\end{array}\right]~\!.

Finally,
from the matrix
{\bf M}_{\rm G\to S}

given in Eq. (
A.3.2
),
one can obtain the
{\bf X}_{\rm G}
– and
{\bf Z}_{\rm G}
–axes
in the Ecliptic coordinate system as

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{\rm G,S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm G\to S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}1\\
0\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.05495\\
0.99374\\
-0.09723\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf Z}_{\rm G,S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm G\to S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0\\
0\\
1\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c}0.86769\\
0.00055\\
0.49711\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!.

These give in turn
the right ascensions and the declinations of
the Galactic Center and the Galactic North Pole
in the Ecliptic coordinate system as

\phi_{\rm GC,S}=86.84^{\circ}=5.79^{\rm h}~\!,

(A125a)

\theta_{\rm GC,S}=-5.58^{\circ}~\!,

(A125b)

and

\phi_{\rm GNP,S}=0.036^{\circ}=2.17^{\rm m}~\!,

(A126a)

\theta_{\rm GNP,S}=29.81^{\circ}~\!,

(A126b)

respectively.

A.3.3
Direction of the movement of the Solar system
around the Galactic center

Figure A5
:

The same as in Fig.
3
(b),
except that
the additional (golden) arrows here
indicate the direction of the movement of the Solar system
around the Galactic center.
Note that
the moving direction of the Solar system
is not parallel to,
but only approximately along
the
{\bf Y}_{\rm G}
–axis
(with an included angle of 8.87
∘
= 35.48
m
),
nor on the (approximate)
{\bf X}_{\rm G}-{\bf Y}_{\rm G}
(Galactic) plane
(0.60
∘
above).

In Fig.
A5
,
we add the (golden) arrow
to indicate
the direction of the movement of the Solar system
around the Galactic center
(cf. Fig.
3
(b)).
The Galactic movement of the Solar system
pointing currently towards the CYGNUS constellation
can be given
in the Equatorial coordinate system as

[
64
]

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\sin\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}=v_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-0.47069\\
0.57508\\
0.66913\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}\cong\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-103.55~{\rm km/s}\\
126.52~{\rm km/s}\\
147.21~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!.

Here we have used

v_{\rm\odot,G}\simeq 220~{\rm km/s}~\!,

(A138)

and
the right ascension and the declination of
the direction of the CYGNUS constellation
in the Equatorial coordinate system are

[
64
]

\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}=8.62^{\rm h}=129.30^{\circ}~\!,

(A139a)

and

\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}=42^{\circ}~\!.

(A139b)

Moreover,
by using the transformation matrices

{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to S}
and
{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to G}

given in Eqs. (
A62b
) and (
A100a
),
the moving direction of the Solar system
in the Ecliptic and the Galactic coordinate systems
can be obtained as

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to S}~\!{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,Eq}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\cos\psi_{\oplus}\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}-\sin\psi_{\oplus}\sin\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\sin\psi_{\oplus}\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}+\cos\psi_{\oplus}\sin\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-0.47069\\
0.26203\\
0.84249\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cong

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-103.55~{\rm km/s}\\
57.65~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,G}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm Eq\to G}~\!{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,Eq}=v_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}0.15262\\
0.98823\\
0.01054\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm G}\cong\left[\begin{array}[]{c}33.58~{\rm km/s}\\
217.41~{\rm km/s}\\
2.32~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm G}~\!.

These give in turn
the right ascension and the declination of
the moving direction of the Solar system
in the Ecliptic coordinate system
as

\phi_{\rm Cyg,S}=150.90^{\circ}=10.06^{\rm h}~\!,

(A159a)

\theta_{\rm Cyg,S}=57.40^{\circ}~\!.

(A159b)

And,
in the Galactic coordinate system,
one has

\phi_{\rm Cyg,G}=81.22^{\circ}=5.41^{\rm h}~\!,

(A160a)

\theta_{\rm Cyg,G}=0.60^{\circ}~\!.

(A160b)

A.3.4
Annual modulation of the Earth’s velocity
in the Ecliptic coordinate system

In this and the next subsections,
we discuss
the annual modulation of the Earth’s velocity
relative to the Dark Matter halo
in the Ecliptic and the Equatorial coordinate systems
separately.
Remind that
our calculations are based on two assumptions.
First,
the Earth’s orbit around the Sun is perfectly circular
on the Ecliptic plane
and the orbital speed is thus a constant.
Second,
the date of the vernal equinox is fixed exactly
at the end of the 79th day (the May 20th) of a 365-day year
and the few extra hours in an actual Solar year
has been neglected.

We begin with four normal seasons,
in which
the orbital velocity of the Earth’s rotation around the Sun
in the Ecliptic coordinate system
on the central date of each season
is along the
{\bf X}_{\rm S}
– or the
{\bf Y}_{\rm S}
–axis,
corresponding to the Earth’s positions
shown in Fig.
9
(a).
By setting
\psi_{\rm yr}=0
,
\pi/2
,
\pi
, and
3\pi/2
,
or,
equivalently,

t=79.0
(the
beginning
of the
21st
of March),
170.25 (June 20th),
261.50 (September 19th),
and 352.75 day (December 19th),
respectively,
into the expression for
the Earth’s orbital velocity around the Sun
given in Eq. (
A.2.3
),
we have the Earth’s velocities in four normal seasons
in the Ecliptic coordinate system as

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,79.00}=v_{\rm\oplus,S}{\bf Y}_{\rm S}~\!,

(A161a)

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,170.25}=-v_{\rm\oplus,S}{\bf X}_{\rm S}~\!,

(A161b)

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,261.50}=-v_{\rm\oplus,S}{\bf Y}_{\rm S}~\!,

(A161c)

and

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,352.75}=v_{\rm\oplus,S}{\bf X}_{\rm S}~\!,

(A161d)

respectively.
By combining with the velocity of the Solar system
given in Eq. (
A.3.3
)
and taking the Earth’s orbital speed
given in Eq. (
A83
),
one can obtain the Earth’s velocities
relative to the Dark Matter halo
in four normal seasons
in the Ecliptic coordinate system
as

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,79.00}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,79.00}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-103.55~{\rm km/s}\\
87.43~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,170.25}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,170.25}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-133.34~{\rm km/s}\\
57.65~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,261.50}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,261.50}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-103.55~{\rm km/s}\\
27.86~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,352.75}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,352.75}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-73.77~{\rm km/s}\\
57.65~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

respectively.

On the other hand,
as shown in Fig.
9
(b),
the Earth’s velocity
relative to the Dark Matter halo
should be maximal (minimal),
once the Earth’s velocity
is (anti–)parallel to the projection of
the moving direction of the Solar system
on the Ecliptic plane.
This requires that,
from Eq. (
A159a
),

\psi_{\rm yr}=60.90^{\circ}=0.3383\pi=1.0628~\!,

(A175)

or,
equivalently,

t_{\rm max}=140.74~{\rm day}~\!,

(A176)

i.e.,
around the 21st of May.
Then,
by setting
\psi_{\rm yr}=1.0628
, 4.2044,
-0.5080
, and 2.6336
(corresponding to the Earth’s positions
in four advanced seasons
shown in Fig.
9
(b)),
or,
equivalently,

t=140.74
(May 21st),
323.24 (November 20th),
49.49 (February 19th),
and 231.99 day (August 20th),
respectively,
into the expression (
A.2.3
),
we have the Earth’s velocities as

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,140.74}=v_{\rm\oplus,S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-0.874\\
0.486\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-26.02~{\rm km/s}\\
14.49~{\rm km/s}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

(A177a)

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,323.24}=v_{\rm\oplus,S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}0.874\\
-0.486\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}26.02~{\rm km/s}\\
-14.49~{\rm km/s}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

(A177b)

{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,49.49}=v_{\rm\oplus,S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}0.486\\
0.874\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}14.49~{\rm km/s}\\
26.02~{\rm km/s}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

(A177c)

and

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,231.99}=v_{\rm\oplus,S}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-0.486\\
-0.874\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-14.49~{\rm km/s}\\
-26.02~{\rm km/s}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

respectively.
Then
the maximal, the minimal and the middle values of
the Earth’s velocities
relative to the Dark Matter halo
in four advanced seasons
can be obtained as

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,140.74}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,140.74}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-129.58~{\rm km/s}\\
72.14~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,323.24}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,323.24}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-77.53~{\rm km/s}\\
43.16~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,49.49}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,49.49}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-89.06~{\rm km/s}\\
83.67~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,231.99}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S,231.99}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-118.04~{\rm km/s}\\
31.62~{\rm km/s}\\
185.35~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!,

respectively.
One can thus estimate the magnitudes and the directions
(the right ascensions and the declinations)
of the Earth’s velocity
relative to the Dark Matter halo
in four normal and four advanced seasons
straightforwardly,
which
we summarize
in Table
A1

for readers’ reference.

Furthermore,
one can in general have that

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf v}_{\rm\odot,S}+{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus,S}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\cos\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}-\left(\frac{v_{\rm\oplus,S}}{v_{\rm\odot,G}}\right)\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\\
\displaystyle\cos\psi_{\oplus}\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}-\sin\psi_{\oplus}\sin\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}+\left(\frac{v_{\rm\oplus,S}}{v_{\rm\odot,G}}\right)\cos\psi_{\rm yr}\\
\sin\psi_{\oplus}\cos\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\sin\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}+\cos\psi_{\oplus}\sin\theta_{\rm Cyg,Eq}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle-0.47069-\left(\frac{v_{\rm\oplus,S}}{v_{\rm\odot,G}}\right)\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\\
\displaystyle 0.26203+\left(\frac{v_{\rm\oplus,S}}{v_{\rm\odot,G}}\right)\cos\psi_{\rm yr}\\
0.84249\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm S}~\!.

Hence,
we can get that

\displaystyle v_{\rm\oplus\chi}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[1+\left(\frac{v_{\rm\oplus,S}}{v_{\rm\odot,G}}\right)^{2}+2\left(\frac{v_{\rm\oplus,S}}{v_{\rm\odot,G}}\right)\Big(0.47069\sin\psi_{\rm yr}+0.26203\cos\psi_{\rm yr}\Big)\right]^{1/2}

(A205)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721ptv_{\rm\odot,G}\left[\alpha_{v,0}+\alpha_{v,1}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi(t-t_{\rm max})}{1~{\rm yr}}\right)+\alpha_{v,2}\cos\left(\frac{2\cdot 2\pi(t-t_{\rm max})}{1~{\rm yr}}\right)+\cdots\right]~\!.

Here,
in the last line,
we expand
v_{\rm\oplus\chi}(t)

by the Fourier cosine series
around the date of the maximal Earth’s relative velocity

t_{\rm max}=140.74
day.
By some numerical calculations,
one can find easily that

\alpha_{v,0}=1.0078~\!,

(A206a)

\alpha_{v,1}=0.0724~\!,

(A206b)

and

\alpha_{v,2}=-0.0013~\!.

(A206c)

Hence,
the time–dependent Earth’s speed
relative to the Dark Matter halo
can be expressed as

v_{\rm\oplus\chi}(t)=v_{\rm\odot,G}\left[1.0078+0.0724\cos\left(\frac{2\pi(t-t_{\rm max})}{1~{\rm yr}}\right)-0.0013\cos\left(\frac{2\cdot 2\pi(t-t_{\rm max})}{1~{\rm yr}}\right)\right]~\!.

(A207)

In Figs.
A6
,
we show
the fitted
v_{\rm\oplus\chi}(t)

of the order of 1 (upper)
and 2 (below),
respectively.

Figure A6
:

Fourier cosine fitting of
the time–dependent Earth’s speed
relative to the Dark Matter halo

v_{\rm\oplus\chi}(t)

of the order of 1 (upper)
and 2 (below),
respectively.
The date of the maximal Earth’s relative velocity is

t_{\rm max}=140.74
day.

A.3.5
Annual modulation of the Earth’s velocity
in the Equatorial coordinate system

First,
by using the transformation matrix
from the Ecliptic
to the Equatorial coordinate system

{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}

given in Eq. (
A.2.2
),
the Earth’s velocities
relative to the Dark Matter halo
in four normal seasons
in the Equatorial coordinate system
can be calculated directly as

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,Eq,79.00}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}~\!{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,79.00}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-103.55~{\rm km/s}\\
153.85~{\rm km/s}\\
135.38~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,Eq,170.25}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}~\!{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,170.25}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-133.34~{\rm km/s}\\
126.52~{\rm km/s}\\
147.21~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,Eq,261.50}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}~\!{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,261.50}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-103.55~{\rm km/s}\\
99.18~{\rm km/s}\\
159.04~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,Eq,352.75}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt{\bf M}_{\rm S\to Eq}~\!{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,S,352.75}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-73.77~{\rm km/s}\\
126.52~{\rm km/s}\\
147.21~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

respectively.

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

150.40~{\rm km/s}\\

\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

and

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{\rm\oplus\chi,Eq,231.99}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

102.63~{\rm km/s}\\
157.54~{\rm km/s}\\
\end{array}\right]_{\rm Eq}~\!,

respectively.

and

for readers’ reference.

A.3.6
Dates considered for demonstrating
the diurnal modulation of the angular WIMP velocity distribution

At the end of this section,
we calculate the dates
considered for demonstrating
the diurnal modulation of
the angular WIMP velocity distribution
shown in Sec.
3.5.1

and Appendix
B
.

From Fig.
A3

and Eq. (
A68a
),
one can have

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{\rm E}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\cos\psi_{\rm PM}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\sin\psi_{\rm PM}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}

(A234)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.59721pt\gamma\cos\psi_{\rm yr}~\!{\bf X}_{\rm Eq}+\gamma\sin\psi_{\rm yr}\cos\psi_{\oplus}~\!{\bf Y}_{\rm Eq}~\!.

Then,
by taking
\psi_{\rm PM}=\phi_{\rm Cyg,Eq}=129.30^{\circ}

given in Eq. (
A139a
),
the specified angle
\psi_{\rm yr}
can be solved directly as

\psi_{\rm yr}=\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\tan\psi_{\rm PM}}{\cos\psi_{\oplus}}\right)=126.9^{\circ}~\!.

(A235)

This means in turn that
the date
on which
the WIMP wind points straightly to the Prime Meridian
in the night is
19
19

19

Remind that
these dates are only pure theoretical estimations
under some simplified assumptions.
It is obviously that
the Prime Meridian should be in the day (night)
on the 207.66 (25.16) day,
respectively.

126.9^{\circ}\left(\frac{365~{\rm day}}{360^{\circ}}\right)+79.0~{\rm day}=207.66~{\rm day}~\!.

(A236)

And the date
on which
the WIMP wind points straightly to the Prime Meridian in the day
is 25.16 day.

Appendix B
Angular distributions of the 3-D WIMP velocity
observed at the known underground laboratories

For readers’ reference,
we summarize
in this section
the angular distributions of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in two laboratory–dependent
(horizontal and laboratory)
coordinate systems
at the locations of several underground laboratories.
Due to their geographical advantages,
two under–constructed laboratories
have also been considered:
the Agua Negra Deep Experiment Site (ANDES)
on the border between Argentina and Chile
as the second underground laboratory
in the Southern Hemisphere
and
the Callio Laboratory located
at the Pyhäsalmi Mine in Finnland,
which will be
the northernmost (close to the Arctic Circle) underground laboratory
in the future
(the Boulby Laboratory is so far the northernmost one).

For each laboratory,
we provide the following figures:

•

the angular distributions of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the horizontal coordinate system
in one entire year
and in the 60-day observation periods
of four advanced seasons on the central date of
49.49 day,
140.74 day,
231.99 day,
and
323.24 day,
respectively;

•

the angular distributions of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the laboratory coordinate system
in one entire year
and in the 60-day observation periods
of four advanced seasons;

•

the angular distributions of the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the laboratory coordinate system
in four 4-hour daily shifts
in two 60-day observation periods
with the central dates
of 207.66 day and 25.16 (= 390.16) day.

Remind that,
firstly,
50 and 500 total WIMP events on average
in (one daily shift of) one observation period
(365 days/year,
60 days/season,
or 4 hours/shift
\times
60 days)
have been simulated.
Secondly,
in the simulations of
the diurnal modulation of
the angular WIMP velocity distribution
presented here,
we have re–calculated the local time
of each simulated event
of the considered laboratory
from the generated measuring UTC time.
Moreover,
by comparing the angular distribution patterns
observed at different laboratories,
it can be found that,
with
\cal O
(500) total WIMP events
and a higher analysis resolution,
we might even be able to demonstrate
a more detailed latitude–dependent distribution pattern.

For the identification of
the annual modulation of
the angular distribution pattern
with real experimental data
in the future,
as the first confirmation of
the directionality of the WIMP wind,
we also summarize
the directions of the simulated 3-D WIMP velocity
with the highest event numbers
(
>
4 times of the all–sky average value)
in the
horizontal
coordinate system
in one entire year
and in four advanced seasons
in one table for each laboratory.
Note that
in these tables
we mark the ranges with a double–underline
to indicate that,
with
\cal O
(500) total WIMP events
and a higher analysis resolution,
some extra bins would exceed the high–WIMP–flux areas
identified with only
\cal O
(50) total events
and a lower resolution.

Appendix C
Annual modulation of the radial WIMP velocity distribution
in four normal seasons

For the sake of completeness and readers’ reference,
in this section,
we provide the reconstruction results of
the annual modulation of
the radial distribution of
the 3-D WIMP velocity
in the Equatorial coordinate system
in the observation periods of four normal seasons.
50 and 500 total events on average
in each 60-day observation period
have been simulated.

C.1
With the one–parameter velocity distribution

f_{1,{\rm sh},v_{0}}(v;v_{0})

As in Sec.
4.3.1
,
we start our reconstruction
by using the one–parameter velocity distribution

f_{1,{\rm sh},v_{0}}(v;v_{0})

given by Eq. (
30
)
with
v_{0}
as the fitting parameter
scanned in the ranges of

160 km/s

\leq v_{0}\leq
270 km/s (50 events) and
190 km/s
\leq v_{0}\leq
240 km/s (500 events)
as well as
the constraint that

v_{\rm e}=1.05~\!v_{0}
.

(a)

49.0 – 109.0 day

(b)

140.25 – 200.25 day

(c)

231.50 – 291.50 day

(d)

322.75 – 382.75 day

Figure A73
:

As in Figs.
30
,
reconstructed with the one–parameter velocity distribution

f_{1,{\rm sh},v_{0}}(v;v_{0})
,
except that
four 60-day observation periods of the normal seasons
have been considered.

(a)

49.0 – 109.0 day

(b)

140.25 – 200.25 day

(c)

231.50 – 291.50 day

(d)

322.75 – 382.75 day

Figure A74
:

As in Figs.
A73
,
except that
500 total events on average
in one 60-day observation period
have been simulated.
Remind that
the scanning range of the fitting parameter
v_{0}

is shrunk to between 190 km/s and 240 km/s.

Appendix D
Analytic forms of the shift Maxwellian velocity distribution

In this section,
we show the plots of
the exact analytic expression of
the shift Maxwellian velocity distribution

f_{1,{\rm sh},{\rm vesc}}(v)

given in Eq. (
)
and the simplified form

f_{1,{\rm sh}}(v)

given in Eq. (
28
).
The Galactic escape velocity
has been
set as
v_{\rm esc}=500
km/s
in Figs.
A81

and
raised to
v_{\rm esc}=600
km/s

in Figs.
A82

[
65
]
.
Additionally,
as a comparison of the shape difference
between two expressions,
their boundaries
due to the annual modulation of
the Earth’s Galactic velocity
v_{\rm e}(t)

given by Eq. (
27
)
have also been given.

It can be seen clearly that,
with the Galactic escape velocity of
v_{\rm esc}=500
km/s,
the shape difference between
the exact analytic expression of
the shift Maxwellian velocity distribution

f_{1,{\rm sh},{\rm vesc}}(v)

and the simplified form
f_{1,{\rm sh}}(v)

is much smaller than
the annual variation of two expressions.
This tiny difference
can also be neglected
compared to
the much larger statistical uncertainties
on the recorded WIMP events
(shown in e.g., Fig.
25
).
Moreover,
once the Galactic escape velocity
would be as large as
v_{\rm esc}=600
km/s,
the tiny shape difference
between two expressions
could even vanish.

Figure A81
:

The exact analytic form of
the shift Maxwellian velocity distribution

f_{1,{\rm sh},{\rm vesc}}(v)

given in Eq. (
)
(dash–dotted blue)
and the simplified form

f_{1,{\rm sh}}(v)

given in Eq. (
28
)
(solid red)
with
the Galactic escape velocity of
v_{\rm esc}=500
km/s.
In the lower frame,
the boundaries of two velocity distributions
due to the annual modulation of
the Earth’s Galactic velocity
v_{\rm e}(t)

given by Eq. (
27
)
have been additionally given
as the dashed blue and the dotted red curves
for the exact and the simplified expressions,
respectively.

Figure A82
:

As in Figs.
A81
,
except that
the Galactic escape velocity
has been raised to
v_{\rm esc}=600
km/s
.
